Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Three middle values miss both limits

A fictional tester explains a quantity-validation selection.

  1. Developer: We tested five, ten, and fifteen successfully. Why add more quantity cases?
  2. Tester: Those all sample the accepted interior. They don't exercise the boundaries at one and twenty.
  3. Developer: Which neighboring rejected values would help check the stated whole-number rule?
  4. Tester: Zero and twenty-one, alongside the accepted endpoints. They cross both specified transitions.
  5. Developer: Then another middle value adds less new rule coverage than those boundary values.
  6. Tester: For this rule, yes. That selection doesn't prove every interior value behaves correctly, though.
  7. Developer: What about one point five? It's numerically inside the interval between one and twenty.
  8. Tester: The rule specifies whole numbers. Decimal handling is an ambiguity needing its own expected result.
  9. Developer: I'll keep that question separate instead of calling the decimal case passed or failed by assumption.
  10. Tester: Good. We have justified boundary checks and an explicit gap in the input contract.
A partition is a reasoned group, not a random sample

Fictional testers explain an input grouping for a document type field.

  1. Junior tester: I chose three unsupported document types at random. Does that count as three different equivalence partitions?
  2. Test lead: Not necessarily. What rule predicts different treatment for those values in this feature?
  3. Junior tester: The requirement rejects every unsupported type with the same type-not-supported result.
  4. Test lead: Then they may belong to one rejected partition for that rule, subject to other relevant differences.
  5. Junior tester: So sampling more names within that group doesn't automatically cover a new behavior category.
  6. Test lead: Exactly. Accepted types and a missing type may involve different rules worth considering separately.
  7. Junior tester: The missing-type rule says required field, while unsupported means a supplied value isn't accepted.
  8. Test lead: That gives you a useful distinction between absence and an unrecognized value.
  9. Junior tester: I'll explain my selection by expected treatment rather than claiming each sample creates another partition.
  10. Test lead: And we'll still investigate specific values when implementation risk suggests the grouping might hide a difference.
A rule depends on two conditions

Fictional testers use a decision table to clarify permission examples.

  1. Tester: The examples cover editors and viewers, but editing also depends on whether the record is archived.
  2. Requirement owner: Correct. Editors can change active records, while archived records are read-only for both roles.
  3. Tester: Then role alone isn't enough to choose expected results. We need the two-condition combinations.
  4. Requirement owner: A decision table would make that explicit: role and archive state determine whether editing is allowed.
  5. Tester: Editor with active means allowed; editor with archived means denied. Is that right?
  6. Requirement owner: Yes. Viewers are denied editing in either archive state under the agreed rule.
  7. Tester: That gives us four combinations with a stated expectation for each, not two loosely described roles.
  8. Requirement owner: Please distinguish a denied edit from a missing record; the record should remain readable here.
  9. Tester: I'll include that visible result in the negative tests, rather than merely check that no change occurred.
  10. Requirement owner: Good. The table now expresses both permission and the expected read-only experience.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Five, ten, and fifteen never touch the limits
  1. Read the six proposed boundary values in order, with their expected accept or reject results.
  2. Swap roles for turns 9-16. Stress should accept rather than passed, and keep decimal handling unresolved.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Read the limits literally

Complete the expected-result exchange. These expectations come from the stated rule, not from completed test runs.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Tester: "The accepted lower endpoint is ___."

Answer and explanation

A. 1

One is explicitly included as the lower endpoint of the range.

2. Developer: "The accepted upper endpoint is ___."

Answer and explanation

A. 20

Twenty is explicitly included as the upper endpoint of the range.

3. Tester: "The whole number immediately above the range is ___."

Answer and explanation

D. 21

Twenty-one is the nearest whole number above the accepted upper endpoint.

4. Developer: "Decimal handling remains ___."

Answer and explanation

D. unspecified

The stated rule provides no handling policy for decimal input.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Course: Software Quality Assurance and Testing English
Professional audience: Software quality assurance analysts, manual testers, test engineers, automation engineers, and test leads
Scope: All products, builds, requirements, observations, and decisions are fictional. This book teaches workplace English, not certification content or a complete testing method. A proposed check has no result until executed, and an observed result applies only to its stated conditions.
Lesson 03: Choosing cases at boundaries and across rules
Original fictional case: A fictional quantity form accepts whole numbers from 1 through 20 inclusive and rejects whole-number values outside that range. The proposed tests use only 5, 10, and 15. Tester Rosa and developer Amir review the gap. They propose boundary-focused values 0, 1, 2, 19, 20, and 21. Decimal handling is unspecified and outside the stated whole-number rule. The new tests are proposed, not executed results.
Language workshop: Five, ten, and fifteen never touch the limits
Communication goal: Explain missing boundary and rejection coverage while keeping unspecified decimal behavior separate from the whole-number rule.
Language guidance: One and twenty are accepted endpoints. Zero and twenty-one are adjacent rejected whole-number values. Two and nineteen check just inside the range. The existing five, ten, and fifteen do not test these edges.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The rule concerns whole-number quantities. / One through twenty are accepted, including both endpoints. / Five, ten, and fifteen are interior values. / One should be accepted. / Zero should be rejected. / Twenty should be accepted. / Twenty-one should be rejected. / Two and nineteen check just inside the limits. / The current set never reaches either boundary. / Each added value tests a specific edge condition. / These are expected outcomes, not completed test results. / Decimal handling is not specified. / We have no agreed rounding rule. / Ask for the decimal policy separately. / Boundary cases improve coverage but do not prove the form defect-free. / Add the six boundary-focused cases and retain their expected outcomes.
Vocabulary: boundary value: Input at an edge where a rule changes. / boundary value analysis: Test design concentrating on rule edges and nearby values. / equivalence partition: Group of inputs expected to follow the same relevant rule. / valid partition: Input group accepted under the specified rule. / invalid partition: Input group rejected under the specified rule. / lower bound: Smallest value within a defined range. / upper bound: Largest value within a defined range. / inclusive range: Range containing both stated endpoints. / whole number: Number without a fractional part in the stated quantity context. / adjacent value: Nearest relevant value beside a boundary. / interior value: Input strictly between the relevant endpoints. / off-by-one error: Mistake that shifts a count or boundary by one unit. / acceptance region: Set of inputs the stated rule accepts. / rejection region: Set of inputs the stated rule rejects. / input domain: Set of inputs relevant to the defined problem. / decimal input: Input containing a fractional numeric value. / validation message: User-facing message explaining an input condition. / test case: Defined input, action, and expected outcome for a check. / case selection: Choice of checks to cover relevant behavior. / coverage item: Rule, value, or condition counted in a coverage assessment. / decision rule: Condition-to-outcome relationship. / decision table: Organized mapping of condition combinations to outcomes. / out-of-range value: Input below or above the accepted range. / unspecified behavior: Behavior for which no governing rule is supplied.
Speaking task: Read the six proposed boundary values in order, with their expected accept or reject results. Swap roles for turns 9-16. Stress should accept rather than passed, and keep decimal handling unresolved. Compare the final proposal with the brief: both endpoints and immediate neighbors, with no invented execution result.
Partner: Rosa: Tester reviewing case selection / Amir: Developer discussing the rule
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Complete the expected-result exchange. These expectations come from the stated rule, not from completed test runs.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Rosa: We can retain useful interior cases, but their count won't establish edge coverage. Three central examples still miss both endpoints.
Amir: Correct. Case selection is about the conditions exercised, not simply having several numbers in a list. The current values all occupy the interior.
Rosa: I'll submit the six cases and expected outcomes as a proposal, not a pass report. The separate decimal question remains open.
Amir: That is accurate coverage language. It improves the design while keeping both execution status and the missing decimal rule visible to the team.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

04Explaining coverage and change riskThe account page opens; the changed paths are unchecked+

Your goal

Explain limited smoke-test evidence and justify focused follow-up on both consumers of a changed formatter.

This lesson in the book, page 31 ↗

A Read the situation

The account page opens; the changed paths are unchecked

A fictional account update passes a smoke test that signs in and opens the account page. The update changes a formatter used by account-save and by the existing export path. Neither of those paths has been tested. Tester Elena and team lead Sam have one hour for further checking. They need to prioritize evidence around the shared change without claiming full coverage or assuming the existing export path is unaffected.

Elena: Tester / Sam: Team lead

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What did the smoke test cover?

Answer and explanation

B. Sign-in and opening the account page

The supplied smoke test covers only sign-in and opening the account page.

2. Which paths use the changed formatter?

Answer and explanation

A. Account-save and existing export

The formatter is shared by account-save and the existing export path.

3. How much time remains?

Answer and explanation

B. One hour

The scenario allows one hour for additional checking, not unlimited coverage.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Opening the account page does not establish correct account-save formatting, and it does not exercise export. Both use the changed formatter. Existing behavior can need regression checking even when the visible update is described as an account change.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
smoke test
Small check set used to detect major basic failures.pass the smoke test
regression testing
Checking existing behavior for problems introduced by change.prioritize regression testing
change impact
Potential effect of a modification on related behavior.assess change impact
shared component
Component used by more than one path or feature.identify the shared component
formatter
Component converting values into a required representation.trace formatter usage
consumer path
Execution route using a shared component.test the consumer path
account-save path
Route that persists an account update.exercise the account-save path
export path
Route producing output for external use.check the export path
coverage gap
Relevant behavior not yet checked.report the coverage gap
risk-based testing
Selection of tests according to relevant likelihood and impact concerns.propose risk-based testing
test priority
Relative order or urgency assigned to checks.set test priority
timebox
Fixed amount of time allocated to work.use a one-hour timebox
residual risk
Risk remaining after the checks or controls performed.disclose residual risk
test scope
Behavior and conditions included in the checking effort.state the test scope
execution evidence
Record of checks actually run and their results.distinguish execution evidence
untested path
Execution route with no relevant test result yet.identify an untested path
dependency mapping
Identification of relationships between components and consumers.use dependency mapping
functional impact
Effect on what users or systems can do.assess functional impact
test objective
Purpose a particular check is meant to serve.define the test objective
selection rationale
Reason for choosing particular checks.explain the selection rationale
coverage claim
Statement about what the tests have exercised.limit the coverage claim
test summary
Concise account of scope, results, and limits.write the test summary
branch coverage
Proportion of defined control-flow branches exercised by tests.report branch coverage
release recommendation
Evidence-based advice about proceeding with a release.qualify a release recommendation

One hour limits the work, not the meaning of full coverage. Propose focused checks of the changed formatter through both consumer paths, then report what actually ran and any remaining gaps without implying all risks were removed.

C Notice the language

Replace tested with named paths

A short status such as smoke passed can be useful if everyone knows its limits. Name what ran, then connect the changed component to its consumers. A time limit calls for explicit priorities and residual risk, not a larger claim about the same narrow evidence.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
State the narrow success
Sign-in and opening the account page passed.
Name the change
The shared formatter changed.
Identify both consumers
Account-save and export use that formatter.
Expose the gap
Neither consumer path has been tested.
Challenge the label
An account update can still affect export.
Explain the priority
I would focus the next checks on the changed component's consumers.
Name the time limit
We have one hour for further checking.
Keep a proposal provisional
Those checks are planned, not completed.
Request a clear objective
Check the formatted result in each consumer path.
Avoid claiming total coverage
A smoke pass is not full regression coverage.
Preserve the unknown
Export behavior after this change remains unverified.
Report actual execution
List which checks ran and their results.
Report unfinished work
Keep any untested path visible.
Separate evidence and advice
The summary can support a recommendation without proving every risk absent.
Name remaining exposure
There will still be residual risk.
Close with a bounded plan
Prioritize both affected paths and report the limits of the hour's work.
6 language and precision notes
Neither
Makes clear that both relevant consumer paths lack results.
Still affect
Challenges an assumption that an existing path is outside the change.
Would focus
Offers a reasoned proposal without fabricating a completed plan.
After this change
Ties the needed evidence to the modified version.
Not full
Limits the coverage meaning of a smoke pass.
Which ... and their
Requires named checks paired with actual outcomes.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which summary is accurate?

Answer and explanation

B. Smoke passed for sign-in and page opening; save and export remain untested

The summary preserves the exact checked scope and both missing consumer paths.

2. Why include export in follow-up?

Answer and explanation

D. It uses the changed formatter

Shared-component usage creates a relevant change-impact question for the existing export path.

3. What does one hour justify?

Answer and explanation

B. Prioritized checks with disclosed gaps

The timebox limits testing and requires explicit reporting of remaining uncertainty.

4. Which statement confuses plan and evidence?

Answer and explanation

A. Both paths passed because we intend to check them

Intending to run checks provides no evidence that either path has passed.

Check explanations in the book, page 91 ↗

E Conversations

The account page opens; the changed paths are unchecked

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Elena: Tester / Sam: Team lead

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • residual risk
  • selection rationale
  • test objective
  • timebox
  • execution evidence
  • test summary
  • change impact
  • coverage gap
  • shared component
  • smoke test
  1. Elena
    Sign-in and opening the account page passed. I don't want that smoke result reported as account-save or export tested; neither path ran.
  2. Sam
    Agreed. What changed that makes those the next checks? The 1 itself tells us only about those basic actions.
  3. Elena
    The update changes a formatter used by both save and export. The account label on the change doesn't remove export from its impact.
  4. Sam
    Then the 2 gives us a concrete reason to include both. Existing export behavior can regress when its dependency changes.
  5. Elena
    Opening the page doesn't compare the formatted saved value. It also doesn't generate an export whose contents we can check.
  6. Sam
    That's the 3. We have basic access evidence, not results for the two consumers of the changed formatter.
  7. Elena
    We have one hour. I'd focus on each consumer's output, with an explicit expected representation, before spending time on unrelated screens.
  8. Sam
    That is a defensible 4. Explain which changed behavior the checks target rather than just promising more test cases.
  9. Elena
    I can't promise full regression coverage in that hour. I'll keep the uncompleted checks visible if the time runs out.
  10. Sam
    Right. The 5 constrains the work; it doesn't redefine a short check set as full coverage.
  11. Elena
    For export, the rationale is the shared formatter, not evidence that it already fails. I don't have a failure probability to attach.
  12. Sam
    State the 6 relationship without inventing one. A relevant dependency is enough to justify investigating that path.
  13. Elena
    Both proposed checks are still planned. I'll update their statuses only after execution gives us actual results.
  14. Sam
    Keep 7 separate from intention. A planned check doesn't become passed because we expect it to be straightforward.
  15. Elena
    If only one consumer finishes, I'll identify which one and its result. I won't combine it with the other under formatter verified.
  16. Sam
    That preserves the 8. Readers need to know what remains unchecked as well as what the completed case showed.
  17. Elena
    I'll also check the content, not merely that Save or Export can be clicked. Otherwise we'd repeat the smoke-test limitation.
  18. Sam
    Exactly. The 9 is the changed formatting behavior in each path. Make the assertion answer that question.
  19. Elena
    Current update: narrow smoke pass, save and export untested, shared formatter changed, one hour available, focused follow-up proposed.
  20. Sam
    That's an accurate 10. The subsequent evidence can inform a recommendation without implying that every risk or release question is settled.

Answers and explanations
  1. smoke test Turn 2. Smoke test describes the small basic check set, not evidence that every changed behavior has been exercised.
  2. shared component Turn 4. Shared component identifies the formatter used by both account-save and export, linking both paths to the modification.
  3. coverage gap Turn 6. Coverage gap names the untested account-save and export behavior that the narrow smoke test did not exercise.
  4. selection rationale Turn 8. Selection rationale explains why the changed formatter's consumers deserve attention within the limited testing time.
  5. timebox Turn 10. Timebox is the one-hour limit on additional work, which does not expand the meaning of the evidence collected.
  6. change impact Turn 12. Change impact concerns the plausible effect of the modified formatter on both consumers without asserting an unmeasured failure probability.
  7. execution evidence Turn 14. Execution evidence consists of actual performed checks and results, not a planned check list.
  8. residual risk Turn 16. Residual risk is the uncertainty or exposure remaining after the checks actually completed, including unfinished planned work.
  9. test objective Turn 18. Test objective specifies the formatted behavior to verify in each consumer path rather than only opening a screen.
  10. test summary Turn 20. Test summary combines checked scope, actual results, proposed follow-up, and limits without claiming complete coverage or release approval.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Elena: Sign-in and opening the account page passed. I don't want that smoke result reported as account-save or export tested; neither path ran.
  2. Sam: Agreed. What changed that makes those the next checks? The smoke test itself tells us only about those basic actions.
  3. Elena: The update changes a formatter used by both save and export. The account label on the change doesn't remove export from its impact.
  4. Sam: Then the shared component gives us a concrete reason to include both. Existing export behavior can regress when its dependency changes.
  5. Elena: Opening the page doesn't compare the formatted saved value. It also doesn't generate an export whose contents we can check.
  6. Sam: That's the coverage gap. We have basic access evidence, not results for the two consumers of the changed formatter.
  7. Elena: We have one hour. I'd focus on each consumer's output, with an explicit expected representation, before spending time on unrelated screens.
  8. Sam: That is a defensible selection rationale. Explain which changed behavior the checks target rather than just promising more test cases.
  9. Elena: I can't promise full regression coverage in that hour. I'll keep the uncompleted checks visible if the time runs out.
  10. Sam: Right. The timebox constrains the work; it doesn't redefine a short check set as full coverage.
  11. Elena: For export, the rationale is the shared formatter, not evidence that it already fails. I don't have a failure probability to attach.
  12. Sam: State the change impact relationship without inventing one. A relevant dependency is enough to justify investigating that path.
  13. Elena: Both proposed checks are still planned. I'll update their statuses only after execution gives us actual results.
  14. Sam: Keep execution evidence separate from intention. A planned check doesn't become passed because we expect it to be straightforward.
  15. Elena: If only one consumer finishes, I'll identify which one and its result. I won't combine it with the other under formatter verified.
  16. Sam: That preserves the residual risk. Readers need to know what remains unchecked as well as what the completed case showed.
  17. Elena: I'll also check the content, not merely that Save or Export can be clicked. Otherwise we'd repeat the smoke-test limitation.
  18. Sam: Exactly. The test objective is the changed formatting behavior in each path. Make the assertion answer that question.
  19. Elena: Current update: narrow smoke pass, save and export untested, shared formatter changed, one hour available, focused follow-up proposed.
  20. Sam: That's an accurate test summary. The subsequent evidence can inform a recommendation without implying that every risk or release question is settled.
Dialogue in the book, page 37 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

The smoke check missed the changed path

A fictional test lead and tester choose targeted checks within one hour.

  1. Test lead: Sign-in and opening the account page passed. Why not repeat that smoke test before release?
  2. Tester: The formatter changed, and both save and export use it. Neither path was exercised by those checks.
  3. Test lead: We have one hour. Which risk would your proposed selection address first?
  4. Tester: I'd check the changed save behavior, then the older export path for a possible regression.
  5. Test lead: So the rationale is the affected code relationship, not simply two more tests in the total.
  6. Tester: Exactly. The smoke test establishes basic access, while these paths examine consequences of the specific change.
  7. Test lead: Would passing those two give us complete test coverage of the account area?
  8. Tester: No. We should name the checked requirements and identify the other paths still outside the selection.
  9. Test lead: I'll accept that bounded plan and preserve the earlier smoke result as separate evidence.
  10. Tester: And I'll map each new result to its requirement, so traceability shows what was actually evaluated.
Linked does not mean executed

Fictional colleagues distinguish a test's requirement mapping from its execution status.

  1. Reviewer: The requirement has a test linked to it. Can I mark it as verified?
  2. Tester: Not from the link alone. Traceability shows the relationship, but this test hasn't run on the candidate.
  3. Reviewer: I mistook the presence of a test case for a passing result. What evidence is missing?
  4. Tester: The candidate build, execution status, and observed result against the expected behavior.
  5. Reviewer: Then the requirement can be mapped while its current verification status remains unexecuted.
  6. Tester: Yes. That distinction helps us identify preparation progress without overstating product evidence.
  7. Reviewer: Could an older passing run still be useful context for the review?
  8. Tester: It could, provided its build and conditions remain visible and aren't relabeled as this candidate's result.
  9. Reviewer: I'll report linked case available, current execution pending, and older evidence separately identified.
  10. Tester: That keeps the mapping useful without turning an association into proof that the behavior passed.
A high percentage with the wrong denominator

Fictional testers clarify a coverage claim about account requirements.

  1. Manager: The dashboard says ninety percent coverage. Does that mean ninety percent of account behavior works?
  2. Test lead: What does the percentage count? The label alone doesn't tell us the coverage basis.
  3. Manager: It counts requirements with at least one linked test, whether or not that test ran.
  4. Test lead: Then it measures mapping completeness, not passing behavior or depth of checking for those requirements.
  5. Manager: I see. A requirement with one shallow test counts the same as one with several targeted checks.
  6. Test lead: Under that measure, yes. We should explain its definition and show execution results separately.
  7. Manager: Could we call it requirements linked to tests rather than general product coverage?
  8. Test lead: That would be clearer. The remaining ten percent has no linked cases under this measure.
  9. Manager: I'll use that label and avoid presenting the percentage as a probability that the software is correct.
  10. Test lead: Good. A precisely named metric is useful; a broader interpretation would conceal important gaps.

F Say it

  1. Read the narrow smoke result and identify both consumers of the changed formatter.
  2. Swap roles for turns 7-14. Keep the one-hour plan separate from completed execution evidence.

Keep scope, change, and time together

Complete the testing update before the proposed follow-up checks have run.

Answer and explanation

The initial smoke test was limited to those two basic actions.

3. Tester: "Neither account-save nor export has been ___."

Answer and explanation

One hour is the stated time available for additional testing.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Lesson 04: Explaining coverage and change risk
Original fictional case: A fictional account update passes a smoke test that signs in and opens the account page. The update changes a formatter used by account-save and by the existing export path. Neither of those paths has been tested. Tester Elena and team lead Sam have one hour for further checking. They need to prioritize evidence around the shared change without claiming full coverage or assuming the existing export path is unaffected.
Language workshop: The account page opens; the changed paths are unchecked
Communication goal: Explain limited smoke-test evidence and justify focused follow-up on both consumers of a changed formatter.
Language guidance: Opening the account page does not establish correct account-save formatting, and it does not exercise export. Both use the changed formatter. Existing behavior can need regression checking even when the visible update is described as an account change.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Sign-in and opening the account page passed. / The shared formatter changed. / Account-save and export use that formatter. / Neither consumer path has been tested. / An account update can still affect export. / I would focus the next checks on the changed component's consumers. / We have one hour for further checking. / Those checks are planned, not completed. / Check the formatted result in each consumer path. / A smoke pass is not full regression coverage. / Export behavior after this change remains unverified. / List which checks ran and their results. / Keep any untested path visible. / The summary can support a recommendation without proving every risk absent. / There will still be residual risk. / Prioritize both affected paths and report the limits of the hour's work.
Vocabulary: smoke test: Small check set used to detect major basic failures. / regression testing: Checking existing behavior for problems introduced by change. / change impact: Potential effect of a modification on related behavior. / shared component: Component used by more than one path or feature. / formatter: Component converting values into a required representation. / consumer path: Execution route using a shared component. / account-save path: Route that persists an account update. / export path: Route producing output for external use. / coverage gap: Relevant behavior not yet checked. / risk-based testing: Selection of tests according to relevant likelihood and impact concerns. / test priority: Relative order or urgency assigned to checks. / timebox: Fixed amount of time allocated to work. / residual risk: Risk remaining after the checks or controls performed. / test scope: Behavior and conditions included in the checking effort. / execution evidence: Record of checks actually run and their results. / untested path: Execution route with no relevant test result yet. / dependency mapping: Identification of relationships between components and consumers. / functional impact: Effect on what users or systems can do. / test objective: Purpose a particular check is meant to serve. / selection rationale: Reason for choosing particular checks. / coverage claim: Statement about what the tests have exercised. / test summary: Concise account of scope, results, and limits. / branch coverage: Proportion of defined control-flow branches exercised by tests. / release recommendation: Evidence-based advice about proceeding with a release.
Speaking task: Read the narrow smoke result and identify both consumers of the changed formatter. Swap roles for turns 7-14. Keep the one-hour plan separate from completed execution evidence. Repeat the closing update, retaining both untested paths and the limit on coverage.
Partner: Elena: Tester / Sam: Team lead
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Complete the testing update before the proposed follow-up checks have run.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Elena: I'll also check the content, not merely that Save or Export can be clicked. Otherwise we'd repeat the smoke-test limitation.
Sam: Exactly. The test objective is the changed formatting behavior in each path. Make the assertion answer that question.
Elena: Current update: narrow smoke pass, save and export untested, shared formatter changed, one hour available, focused follow-up proposed.
Sam: That's an accurate test summary. The subsequent evidence can inform a recommendation without implying that every risk or release question is settled.
END REFERENCE

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

05Investigating inconsistent automated resultsCleanup deletes what search expects+

Your goal

Describe an order-dependent automated failure and request isolated test data instead of treating reruns as a fix.

This lesson in the book, page 40 ↗

A Read the situation

Cleanup deletes what search expects

On fictional build T8, an automated search check fails when it follows a cleanup check but passes when run alone. The cleanup removes a shared sample record that search expects. No code change occurs between these observations. Automation engineer Noah and tester Aisha compare the starting state and execution order. They need to remove the shared-data interference and verify the revised arrangement rather than simply rerun until the suite appears green.

Noah: Automation engineer / Aisha: Tester

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. When does the search check fail?

Answer and explanation

B. After the cleanup check

The reported failure occurs when cleanup runs before search on T8.

2. What does cleanup remove?

Answer and explanation

C. The shared sample record search expects

Cleanup deletes the same sample record required by the search check.

3. What changes between the observations?

Answer and explanation

B. Order and relevant starting state, not code

No code change occurs; the supplied difference concerns test order and shared data.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

The search result differs with test order because cleanup removes its expected sample record. No code change is needed for this interference to matter. Compare the starting state, not just the build identifier.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
flaky test
Test showing inconsistent outcomes under apparently similar conditions.investigate a flaky test
order dependency
Dependence of a result on the sequence of test execution.expose an order dependency
shared fixture
Prepared data or state used by multiple checks.isolate a shared fixture
setup
Preparation establishing a test's starting conditions.verify setup
teardown
Work restoring or removing state after a test.review teardown
cleanup
Removal of data or resources no longer needed by a check.limit cleanup scope
test isolation
Independence of a test from other tests' state changes.restore test isolation
state leakage
Unintended influence of one test's state on another.detect state leakage
sample record
Prepared data item used for testing.preserve the sample record
precondition
Required state before a check starts.establish the precondition
execution order
Sequence in which checks run.record execution order
standalone run
Execution of a check without the surrounding suite.compare a standalone run
suite run
Execution of a group of related checks.inspect the suite run
rerun
Another execution of an existing check.qualify a rerun result
false alarm
Failure signal not caused by the claimed product defect.investigate a false alarm
failure evidence
Records supporting an observed unsuccessful result.retain failure evidence
test data ownership
Responsibility for creating and removing test records.define test data ownership
unique test data
Data assigned specifically to one test or execution.use unique test data
deterministic setup
Preparation that establishes predictable required conditions.create deterministic setup
environment state
Relevant current conditions in the test setting.compare environment state
interference
Effect of one check on another's operation.remove test interference
quarantine
Temporary separation of an unreliable check under a defined policy.review quarantine status
continuous integration
Frequent integration with automated checking; abbreviated CI.investigate the CI result
verification run
Execution used to check whether a proposed correction works.perform a verification run

A passing standalone rerun is not a repair of the suite. Define data ownership and setup so each check has its required state without deleting another check's data, then verify both the relevant sequence and isolated behavior.

C Notice the language

Inconsistent does not mean meaningless

Different outcomes can become understandable when hidden conditions are named. Record order and data state before calling a test random. A passing rerun can be evidence about changed conditions, but it does not erase the earlier failure or repair isolation.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Identify the build
Both observations are on T8.
Report the failing order
Search fails after cleanup.
Report the comparison
Search passes when run alone.
Preserve the code fact
No code changed between those observations.
Name the shared dependency
Both checks depend on the sample record's state.
Explain cleanup's effect
Cleanup removes the record search expects.
Avoid calling it random
The observed pattern is order-dependent.
Request the starting state
Was the required record present when search began?
Separate rerun and repair
A passing rerun does not fix isolation.
Request ownership
Which check creates and removes this record?
Propose isolation
Give the checks independent data or a defined setup.
Preserve the evidence
Keep the failed sequence and logs.
Avoid suppressing the symptom
Do not remove the assertion merely to get green.
Request verification
Check the revised arrangement in the relevant sequence.
Limit the conclusion
The standalone pass does not prove the suite is reliable.
Close with the cause of interference
Resolve shared-data ownership and verify the resulting behavior.
6 language and precision notes
After
Makes sequence part of the reported condition.
When run alone
Identifies the comparison as a different execution context.
No code changed
Removes one explanation without claiming all conditions were identical.
Required record
Names a specific precondition rather than vague environment trouble.
Does not fix
Distinguishes another observation from an actual correction.
Relevant sequence
Requests verification where the interference was originally observed.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which explanation matches the evidence?

Answer and explanation

D. Cleanup removes data needed by the later search check

The supplied shared-record relationship explains the observed order-dependent interference.

2. What comparison most directly tests the reported interference?

Answer and explanation

B. Record presence before search alone and after cleanup on T8

The difference is the shared record removed by cleanup; matching the build while observing record presence targets that interference.

3. Which action is not a demonstrated fix?

Answer and explanation

B. Rerun alone until the check passes

A standalone pass leaves the original suite interference unresolved.

4. What should verification include?

Answer and explanation

A. The relevant sequence after the data arrangement is corrected

Verification must address the sequence and starting-state problem that produced the failure.

    The shared sample record. Search expects it, and cleanup deletes it. The build matches, but the data at the start doesn't.
  4. Aisha
    That explains an 2. An identical build doesn't make the starting conditions identical.
  5. Noah
    A standalone rerun could turn the report green without fixing the sequence that lost the record. It would hide what we need to investigate.
  6. Aisha
    Exactly. A 3 is another observation, not a repair. Keep its changed context visible instead of replacing the failure with it.
  7. Noah
    We need to decide who creates and removes the sample. Right now cleanup assumes ownership of something search treats as available.
  8. Aisha
    Make 4 explicit. Otherwise a different execution order can expose the same problem again.
  9. Noah
    We could give each check its own records, or establish the required state explicitly without depending on another check's leftovers.
  10. Aisha
    Those address 5. Choose and review the arrangement, then verify it; proposing separation isn't evidence that the suite is repaired.
  11. Noah
    I'll retain the failing order and logs. The successful isolated run belongs beside them as a comparison, not as a replacement.
  12. Aisha
    Good. That 6 shows why the shared state matters. Deleting it would remove the most useful reproduction conditions.
  13. Noah
    The record's presence is required before the search assertion can mean anything. We shouldn't leave that hidden in the environment.
  14. Aisha
    Establish the 7 in setup. A missing fixture and an application search defect are different problems to report.
  15. Noah
    Cleanup also needs narrower ownership checks. A broad delete based on a shared label could still remove another test's record.
  16. Aisha
    That's the 8 to remove. Keep the useful search assertion rather than weakening it until the suite looks green.
  17. Noah
    After the revision, I'll run cleanup then search, as well as search alone, and capture the starting data and actual results.
  18. Aisha
    The 9 needs that original sequence. A standalone pass alone would leave the reported interaction unverified.

    My handoff will show T8, unchanged code, deletion of the expected record, and the two observed outcomes. The revised arrangement is not yet verified.
  20. Aisha
    That names the 10 precisely. We can investigate the actual dependency instead of calling the result random and moving on.

Answers and explanations
  1. execution order Turn 2. Execution order is the relevant sequence in which cleanup precedes the failing search check on the same build.
  2. order dependency Turn 4. Order dependency means the search result depends on cleanup having run first and removed its required record.
  3. rerun Turn 6. Rerun is another execution, whose success does not repair the original interference between cleanup and search.
  4. test data ownership Turn 8. Test data ownership assigns responsibility for creating and removing records so one check does not invalidate another's preconditions.
  5. test isolation Turn 10. Test isolation prevents another check's state changes from determining whether search has its required starting data.
  6. failure evidence Turn 12. Failure evidence preserves the observed failing sequence and its conditions even when a different standalone attempt passes.
  7. precondition Turn 14. Precondition is the required sample-record state that must be established before the search check starts.
  8. interference Turn 16. Interference is cleanup's unintended effect on the later search check through removal of its required record.
  9. verification run Turn 18. Verification run checks the revised data arrangement under the relevant sequence instead of assuming the proposed fix succeeded.
  10. environment state Turn 20. Environment state includes the sample-record presence that changes between suite and standalone execution despite an unchanged build.
Read the complete conversation

Dialogue in the book, page 46 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Cleanup removes the search fixture

A fictional tester and automation engineer explain the T8 suite result.

  1. Tester: The search check passes alone on T8 but fails immediately after the cleanup check.
  2. Automation engineer: Cleanup removes the shared sample record. Does search assume that record already exists?
  3. Tester: Yes. Its fixture depends on the record, but search doesn't establish that starting state independently.
  4. Automation engineer: Then test isolation is broken. The order changes a required precondition without changing the application build.
  5. Tester: That explains this result pattern. It doesn't establish that every product search behavior is correct.
  6. Automation engineer: Agreed. We should preserve the first failure and repair the shared-state assumption in the tests.
  7. Tester: Would rerunning until green conceal the useful clue about which check ran beforehand?
  8. Automation engineer: It would. The sequence is evidence, not just an inconvenient red result to replace.
  9. Tester: I'll label the supported fixture interaction and keep the broader product conclusion limited.
  10. Automation engineer: I'll take the isolation issue and provide a reviewed fixture change for comparable reruns.
A locator fails after a harmless label update

Fictional testers distinguish a broken automated check from the product behavior it intended to examine.

  1. Automation engineer: The automated check reports a missing button, but the screen visibly has the intended control.
  2. Tester: What does the locator use? Did the agreed label change without the test reference changing?
  3. Automation engineer: Yes. The requirement now says Continue, while the check still searches for Next.
  4. Tester: Then this failure doesn't establish the control is absent. The check's expectation is stale.
  5. Automation engineer: I'll confirm the control's function separately rather than assume the visible label proves it works.
  6. Tester: Good. A false positive about absence still leaves the intended interaction needing evaluation.
  7. Automation engineer: The updated label matches the requirement, and the manual interaction reaches the expected next screen.
  8. Tester: That resolves this supplied example, though the automated locator correction still needs review.
  9. Automation engineer: I'll retain the failure's explanation and distinguish the product check from the test-maintenance task.
  10. Tester: And the report won't turn every locator failure into a product defect or dismiss them all as noise.
The rerun used different data

Fictional testers challenge a flaky-test label when the inputs changed.

  1. Test lead: The first run failed and the second passed. Is it a flaky test?
  2. Tester: Not yet. The second run used a different sample account with different saved preferences.
  3. Test lead: Then inconsistent outcomes may reflect different test conditions rather than instability under comparable conditions.
  4. Tester: Exactly. The term flaky shouldn't hide a known input difference that may explain the result.
  5. Test lead: What should the report preserve before we ask the automation engineer to investigate?
  6. Tester: Both account states, the build, the actions, and their actual results against the intended expectations.
  7. Test lead: Can we repeat the original fixture without substituting the easier passing account?
  8. Tester: Yes. The invented fixture is available, so a matching attempt can be requested.
  9. Test lead: Let's describe the current evidence as two noncomparable runs, with the first failure still open.
  10. Tester: Agreed. We haven't established either a product correction or a test-isolation problem from those runs alone.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Course: Software Quality Assurance and Testing English
Professional audience: Software quality assurance analysts, manual testers, test engineers, automation engineers, and test leads
Scope: All products, builds, requirements, observations, and decisions are fictional. This book teaches workplace English, not certification content or a complete testing method. Actual requirements, test policies, evidence standards, privacy safeguards, and release authority take precedence. The exercises authorize no live-system changes. A proposed check has no result until executed, and an observed result applies only to its stated conditions.
Lesson 05: Investigating inconsistent automated results
Original fictional case: On fictional build T8, an automated search check fails when it follows a cleanup check but passes when run alone. The cleanup removes a shared sample record that search expects. No code change occurs between these observations. Automation engineer Noah and tester Aisha compare the starting state and execution order. They need to remove the shared-data interference and verify the revised arrangement rather than simply rerun until the suite appears green.
Language workshop: Cleanup deletes what search expects
Communication goal: Describe an order-dependent automated failure and request isolated test data instead of treating reruns as a fix.
Language guidance: The search result differs with test order because cleanup removes its expected sample record. No code change is needed for this interference to matter. Compare the starting state, not just the build identifier.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Both observations are on T8. / Search fails after cleanup. / Search passes when run alone. / No code changed between those observations. / Both checks depend on the sample record's state. / Cleanup removes the record search expects. / The observed pattern is order-dependent. / Was the required record present when search began? / A passing rerun does not fix isolation. / Which check creates and removes this record? / Give the checks independent data or a defined setup. / Keep the failed sequence and logs. / Do not remove the assertion merely to get green. / Check the revised arrangement in the relevant sequence. / The standalone pass does not prove the suite is reliable. / Resolve shared-data ownership and verify the resulting behavior.
Vocabulary: flaky test: Test showing inconsistent outcomes under apparently similar conditions. / order dependency: Dependence of a result on the sequence of test execution. / shared fixture: Prepared data or state used by multiple checks. / setup: Preparation establishing a test's starting conditions. / teardown: Work restoring or removing state after a test. / cleanup: Removal of data or resources no longer needed by a check. / test isolation: Independence of a test from other tests' state changes. / state leakage: Unintended influence of one test's state on another. / sample record: Prepared data item used for testing. / precondition: Required state before a check starts. / execution order: Sequence in which checks run. / standalone run: Execution of a check without the surrounding suite. / suite run: Execution of a group of related checks. / rerun: Another execution of an existing check. / false alarm: Failure signal not caused by the claimed product defect. / failure evidence: Records supporting an observed unsuccessful result. / test data ownership: Responsibility for creating and removing test records. / unique test data: Data assigned specifically to one test or execution. / deterministic setup: Preparation that establishes predictable required conditions. / environment state: Relevant current conditions in the test setting. / interference: Effect of one check on another's operation. / quarantine: Temporary separation of an unreliable check under a defined policy. / continuous integration: Frequent integration with automated checking; abbreviated CI. / verification run: Execution used to check whether a proposed correction works.
Speaking task: Read the two T8 outcomes with their execution order and starting data state. Swap roles for turns 7-16. Stress ownership of the sample record and preserving the search assertion. Compare the final report with the brief. The data arrangement is proposed, not yet verified.
Partner: Noah: Automation engineer / Aisha: Tester
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Complete the automation report using the observed order and shared-record facts.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Noah: After the revision, I'll run cleanup then search, as well as search alone, and capture the starting data and actual results.
Aisha: The verification run needs that original sequence. A standalone pass alone would leave the reported interaction unverified.
Noah: My handoff will show T8, unchanged code, deletion of the expected record, and the two observed outcomes. The revised arrangement is not yet verified.
Aisha: That names the environment state precisely. We can investigate the actual dependency instead of calling the result random and moving on.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Course: Software Quality Assurance and Testing English
Professional audience: Software quality assurance analysts, manual testers, test engineers, automation engineers, and test leads
Scope: All products, builds, requirements, observations, and decisions are fictional. This book teaches workplace English, not certification content or a complete testing method. Actual requirements, test policies, evidence standards, privacy safeguards, and release authority take precedence. The exercises authorize no live-system changes. A proposed check has no result until executed, and an observed result applies only to its stated conditions.
Lesson 05: Investigating inconsistent automated results
Original fictional case: On fictional build T8, an automated search check fails when it follows a cleanup check but passes when run alone. The cleanup removes a shared sample record that search expects. No code change occurs between these observations. Automation engineer Noah and tester Aisha compare the starting state and execution order. They need to remove the shared-data interference and verify the revised arrangement rather than simply rerun until the suite appears green.
Language workshop: Cleanup deletes what search expects
Communication goal: Describe an order-dependent automated failure and request isolated test data instead of treating reruns as a fix.
Language guidance: The search result differs with test order because cleanup removes its expected sample record. No code change is needed for this interference to matter. Compare the starting state, not just the build identifier.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Both observations are on T8. / Search fails after cleanup. / Search passes when run alone. / No code changed between those observations. / Both checks depend on the sample record's state. / Cleanup removes the record search expects. / The observed pattern is order-dependent. / Was the required record present when search began? / A passing rerun does not fix isolation. / Which check creates and removes this record? / Give the checks independent data or a defined setup. / Keep the failed sequence and logs. / Do not remove the assertion merely to get green. / Check the revised arrangement in the relevant sequence. / The standalone pass does not prove the suite is reliable. / Resolve shared-data ownership and verify the resulting behavior.
Vocabulary: flaky test: Test showing inconsistent outcomes under apparently similar conditions. / order dependency: Dependence of a result on the sequence of test execution. / shared fixture: Prepared data or state used by multiple checks. / setup: Preparation establishing a test's starting conditions. / teardown: Work restoring or removing state after a test. / cleanup: Removal of data or resources no longer needed by a check. / test isolation: Independence of a test from other tests' state changes. / state leakage: Unintended influence of one test's state on another. / sample record: Prepared data item used for testing. / precondition: Required state before a check starts. / execution order: Sequence in which checks run. / standalone run: Execution of a check without the surrounding suite. / suite run: Execution of a group of related checks. / rerun: Another execution of an existing check. / false alarm: Failure signal not caused by the claimed product defect. / failure evidence: Records supporting an observed unsuccessful result. / test data ownership: Responsibility for creating and removing test records. / unique test data: Data assigned specifically to one test or execution. / deterministic setup: Preparation that establishes predictable required conditions. / environment state: Relevant current conditions in the test setting. / interference: Effect of one check on another's operation. / quarantine: Temporary separation of an unreliable check under a defined policy. / continuous integration: Frequent integration with automated checking; abbreviated CI. / verification run: Execution used to check whether a proposed correction works.
Speaking task: Read the two T8 outcomes with their execution order and starting data state. Swap roles for turns 7-16. Stress ownership of the sample record and preserving the search assertion. Compare the final report with the brief. The data arrangement is proposed, not yet verified.
Partner: Noah: Automation engineer / Aisha: Tester
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Complete the automation report using the observed order and shared-record facts.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Noah: After the revision, I'll run cleanup then search, as well as search alone, and capture the starting data and actual results.
Aisha: The verification run needs that original sequence. A standalone pass alone would leave the reported interaction unverified.
Noah: My handoff will show T8, unchanged code, deletion of the expected record, and the two observed outcomes. The revised arrangement is not yet verified.
Aisha: That names the environment state precisely. We can investigate the actual dependency instead of calling the result random and moving on.
END REFERENCE
06Separating impact from scheduling in triageThe missing export row and tomorrow's visible typo+

Your goal

Compare functional impact, reach, and timing without equating demonstration urgency with defect severity or release approval.

This lesson in the book, page 49 ↗

A Read the situation

The missing export row and tomorrow's visible typo

An export defect omits the final row in one monthly workflow. A heading typo appears on every account page. Tomorrow's customer demonstration uses the account page, with no export planned. Tester Lina and product lead Marcus discuss triage. The export has a data-completeness impact; the typo has broad visibility and immediate demonstration relevance. No severity scale, final repair order, or approval to release either issue is supplied.

Lina: Tester / Marcus: Product lead

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What does the export defect do?

Answer and explanation

C. Omits the final row in one monthly workflow

The reported functional effect is a missing final export row in the monthly workflow.

2. What will tomorrow's demonstration use?

Answer and explanation

A. The account page, with no export planned

The demonstration uses the account page, while export is not planned.

3. What decision has already been made?

Answer and explanation

A. No final repair order or release approval is supplied

The scenario provides discussion facts but no final scheduling or release decision.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Monthly describes when the export workflow occurs; it does not make a missing row trivial. Every account page describes the typo's reach; it does not make its functional impact identical to missing export data.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
triage
Structured review to classify issues and decide next handling.bring a defect to triage
severity
Degree of impact caused by a defect under the relevant scale.assess severity
priority
Relative urgency or order assigned to work.set priority
functional impact
Effect on the ability to perform the intended task.explain functional impact
visibility
Extent to which users encounter or notice an issue.describe visibility
reach
Range of users or surfaces affected.distinguish reach from harm
frequency
How often the relevant event or workflow occurs.state workflow frequency
data completeness
Presence of all required data items.verify data completeness
cosmetic defect
Presentation issue without an established functional failure here.classify a cosmetic defect carefully
customer demonstration
Planned presentation of product behavior to a customer.prepare for the customer demonstration
business timing
Importance arising from an event or deadline.explain business timing
repair order
Sequence in which issues are scheduled for correction.agree the repair order
release blocker
Issue preventing release under the applicable criteria.identify a release blocker
waiver
Explicit authorized acceptance of an exception.record a waiver
risk acceptance
Authorized decision to proceed with identified exposure.distinguish risk acceptance
workaround
Alternative way to complete a task despite a defect.verify a workaround
affected workflow
Process in which the reported issue occurs.name the affected workflow
stakeholder impact
Consequence for people who rely on the product.assess stakeholder impact
defect classification
Assignment of an issue to a defined category.explain defect classification
scheduling constraint
Limit affecting when work can be performed.state the scheduling constraint
decision owner
Person responsible for a specified decision.identify the decision owner
evidence-based comparison
Comparison grounded in observed effects and stated context.make an evidence-based comparison
defer
Postpone work without claiming the issue is resolved.document a decision to defer
disposition
Recorded decision about how an issue will be handled.record the disposition

Tomorrow's demonstration creates a timing reason to discuss the typo urgently. It does not automatically set a universal severity ranking, decide the repair order, or approve release with either defect. Those decisions require the actual process and authority.

C Notice the language

Keep two dimensions on the table

Teams can disagree because one person is discussing harm and another is discussing timing. Name each dimension before comparing issues. A visible typo may be urgent for an event without making lost export data harmless, and discussing either issue does not authorize release.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Name the export effect
The export omits its final row.
Limit the affected workflow
The observed issue affects the monthly workflow.
Name the typo's reach
The heading typo appears on every account page.
State tomorrow's scope
The demonstration uses the account page.
Preserve the exclusion
No export is planned for the demonstration.
Separate dimensions
Impact and scheduling urgency are different questions.
Avoid minimizing
Monthly use does not make missing data harmless.
Avoid inflating
Broad visibility does not automatically mean greater functional harm.
Explain timing
The demonstration makes the typo immediately relevant.
Request the scale
Which severity definitions apply here?
Keep the decision open
We have not agreed the repair order.
Avoid implying permission
Triage discussion is not release approval.
Request a recorded decision
Please record the disposition for each issue.
Avoid inventing a workaround
We have not established an alternative export method.
Preserve both concerns
Keep data completeness and presentation visibility in the comparison.
Close with the distinction
Discuss impact, timing, and authorization separately.
6 language and precision notes
Affects
Names the observed consequence without inventing additional damage.
Does not make
Rejects an invalid inference from frequency or reach.
Immediately relevant
Expresses timing without assigning a severity category.
Which definitions
Requests the actual scale instead of assuming a universal one.
Have not agreed
Keeps scheduling unresolved.
For each issue
Prevents one broad statement from concealing different dispositions.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which comparison is accurate?

Answer and explanation

A. Export loses a row; the typo is widely visible and relevant to tomorrow's demo

The correct comparison preserves functional impact, reach, and event timing separately.

2. What does the demonstration establish?

Answer and explanation

B. A timing reason to discuss the typo urgently

The planned account-page demonstration affects scheduling relevance, not defect correctness or release authority.

3. Which claim needs more evidence?

Answer and explanation

B. A workaround exists for the export

No alternative export method or verified workaround is supplied.

4. Which statement avoids a false decision?

Answer and explanation

D. The repair order and release disposition still need agreement

The scenario gives no final scheduling or authorization decision.
