E Conversations

The missing export row and tomorrow's visible typo

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Lina: Tester / Marcus: Product lead

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • Priority
  • disposition
  • business timing
  • Reach
  • risk acceptance
  • evidence-based comparison
  • defect classification
  • functional impact
  • workaround
  • Frequency
  1. Lina
    Before we rank these issues, can we separate missing export data from the heading typo? Tomorrow's demonstration affects urgency, but not the underlying consequences.
  2. Marcus
    Start with the 1 of the export. A missing row affects the completeness of that workflow's output, even though the workflow runs monthly.
  3. Lina
    Monthly is the workflow frequency, not proof that the missing row is harmless. We should retain the actual completeness problem in the description.
  4. Marcus
    Agreed. 2 is one part of the context, not a substitute for describing the effect. What is established about the typo?
  5. Lina
    The typo appears on every account page, including the one used in tomorrow's demonstration. No export is planned for that event.
  6. Marcus
    That gives the typo immediate 3. We should discuss it urgently for that reason, while keeping its presentation effect distinct from the export's missing data.
  7. Lina
    So its visibility is broad, while the export has a different functional consequence. I don't want account-wide to become a substitute for an impact analysis.
  8. Marcus
    Yes. 4 and severity are not synonyms. A widespread heading error and an incomplete export have different consequences that need separate descriptions.
  9. Lina
    We haven't been supplied a severity scale. I'll describe the observed consequences before assigning labels such as critical or minor.
  10. Marcus
    Use the applicable 5 process when those labels are assigned. For this discussion, the concrete effects and timing are enough to explain the competing concerns.
  11. Lina
    Does discussing the typo urgently imply the export is cleared to ship? I'd like the meeting note to avoid linking those decisions.
  12. Marcus
    No. 6 concerns what receives attention and when. It is not permission to ship a known issue, and we have not finalized the repair order either.
  13. Lina
    Then each issue needs a recorded outcome once decided: corrected, deferred, or still pending. Handled wouldn't tell the next person which one applies.
  14. Marcus
    Exactly. Record the 7 once the authorized decision is made. Do not write deferred as though it meant corrected.
  15. Lina
    We also don't have a verified alternative export method. I won't soften the risk statement by inventing a workaround.
  16. Marcus
    Correct. A 8 is another factual claim requiring evidence. It cannot be added just to make the risk summary sound more reassuring.
  17. Lina
    My comparison will show the missing row, the typo's reach, and the demonstration's actual scope. It won't invent consequences we haven't observed.
  18. Marcus
    That is an 9. It gives the team a clear basis for scheduling discussion without pretending a decision has already been reached.
  19. Lina
    I'll leave the repair order and release disposition pending. Strongly arguing for urgency doesn't mean I can announce an authorization.
  20. Marcus
    Good. Keep any 10 separate and explicit. We can discuss urgency strongly without silently approving either defect for release.

Answers and explanations
  1. functional impact Turn 2. Functional impact identifies what the export defect prevents or corrupts, here completeness of the exported rows.
  2. Frequency Turn 4. Frequency describes how often the monthly workflow occurs and does not determine the seriousness of a missing row by itself.
  3. business timing Turn 6. Business timing captures the upcoming demonstration's relevance to scheduling without equating it with greater functional harm.
  4. Reach Turn 8. Reach describes the affected surfaces, such as every account page, rather than the degree of functional damage.
  5. defect classification Turn 10. Defect classification should follow the actual agreed categories rather than invented labels based only on frequency or visibility.
  6. Priority Turn 12. Priority concerns urgency or work order and does not itself authorize release with an unresolved defect.
  7. disposition Turn 14. Disposition is the specific handling decision for each issue, which must not be replaced by a vague handled status.
  8. workaround Turn 16. Workaround is an alternative way to complete the task, and none has been established for the incomplete export.
  9. evidence-based comparison Turn 18. Evidence-based comparison uses the stated effects and demonstration scope without inventing severity ratings, workarounds, or approvals.
  10. risk acceptance Turn 20. Risk acceptance is an explicit authorized decision about identified exposure, not an inference from urgency or a triage conversation.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Lina: Before we rank these issues, can we separate missing export data from the heading typo? Tomorrow's demonstration affects urgency, but not the underlying consequences.
  2. Marcus: Start with the functional impact of the export. A missing row affects the completeness of that workflow's output, even though the workflow runs monthly.
  3. Lina: Monthly is the workflow frequency, not proof that the missing row is harmless. We should retain the actual completeness problem in the description.
  4. Marcus: Agreed. Frequency is one part of the context, not a substitute for describing the effect. What is established about the typo?
  5. Lina: The typo appears on every account page, including the one used in tomorrow's demonstration. No export is planned for that event.
  6. Marcus: That gives the typo immediate business timing. We should discuss it urgently for that reason, while keeping its presentation effect distinct from the export's missing data.
  7. Lina: So its visibility is broad, while the export has a different functional consequence. I don't want account-wide to become a substitute for an impact analysis.
  8. Marcus: Yes. Reach and severity are not synonyms. A widespread heading error and an incomplete export have different consequences that need separate descriptions.
  9. Lina: We haven't been supplied a severity scale. I'll describe the observed consequences before assigning labels such as critical or minor.
  10. Marcus: Use the applicable defect classification process when those labels are assigned. For this discussion, the concrete effects and timing are enough to explain the competing concerns.
  11. Lina: Does discussing the typo urgently imply the export is cleared to ship? I'd like the meeting note to avoid linking those decisions.
  12. Marcus: No. Priority concerns what receives attention and when. It is not permission to ship a known issue, and we have not finalized the repair order either.
  13. Lina: Then each issue needs a recorded outcome once decided: corrected, deferred, or still pending. Handled wouldn't tell the next person which one applies.
  14. Marcus: Exactly. Record the disposition once the authorized decision is made. Do not write deferred as though it meant corrected.
  15. Lina: We also don't have a verified alternative export method. I won't soften the risk statement by inventing a workaround.
  16. Marcus: Correct. A workaround is another factual claim requiring evidence. It cannot be added just to make the risk summary sound more reassuring.

Dialogue in the book, page 55 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

The demonstration changes urgency, not the omitted row

A fictional tester facilitates triage of an export defect and a heading typo.

  1. Triage coordinator: Tomorrow's demonstration shows the typo. Should we call it more severe than the export issue?
  2. Tester: Let's separate severity from priority. The export omits a row; the typo affects presentation.
  3. Triage coordinator: The demonstration makes the typo urgent, even though no export is planned during it.
  4. Tester: Yes. That timing can affect repair priority without changing either issue's functional impact.
  5. Triage coordinator: How should we compare the affected scope without making the export problem sound trivial?
  6. Tester: State that it affects one monthly workflow and loses a row; the typo appears on every account page.
  7. Triage coordinator: Then we can discuss impact and scheduling explicitly instead of forcing both into one ranking.
  8. Tester: Exactly. The team can propose an order while leaving the release implications for the responsible reviewer.
  9. Triage coordinator: I'll record the demonstration as the scheduling reason, not as evidence of greater functional damage.
  10. Tester: I'll retain the export's data omission in the risk discussion even if repair happens later.
Matching titles do not establish duplicates

A fictional tester requests evidence before merging two blank-screen reports.

  1. Developer: Both reports are titled blank screen. Can we close the newer one as a duplicate?
  2. Tester: Let's compare builds, reproduction steps, and affected pages before deciding they describe the same defect.
  3. Developer: The first concerns account search after sign-in. The second concerns export preview after selecting a file.
  4. Tester: Those differences matter. A shared symptom doesn't establish a shared cause or an identical failure.
  5. Developer: Could we link them as possibly related without losing the second report's evidence?
  6. Tester: Yes. That preserves the relationship as a hypothesis while keeping each reproduction available.
  7. Developer: What finding would support marking the second as a duplicate rather than merely related?
  8. Tester: A reviewed connection showing both reports describe the same defect, with the distinct evidence retained.
  9. Developer: Then I'll keep both open as possibly related, without labeling either a duplicate report.
  10. Tester: That keeps the record honest while giving the investigating developer a useful lead.
A deferred defect is not a resolved defect

A fictional tester clarifies the status of a postponed display correction.

  1. Coordinator: In defect triage, we postponed the display correction. Should its status become resolved?
  2. Tester: No. Deferral changes when it's addressed, not whether the incorrect behavior has been corrected.
  3. Coordinator: The issue affects a seldom-used report, and the team wants to prioritize a blocking save problem.
  4. Tester: That is a scheduling rationale we can record, while preserving the display defect's actual impact.
  5. Coordinator: Who should own the follow-up so it doesn't vanish after today's triage meeting?
  6. Tester: The reporting maintainer has accepted it. The next prioritization review will reconsider its place.
  7. Coordinator: Can the summary say deferred with an owner and reason, rather than fixed?
  8. Tester: Yes. It should also link the reproduction and current affected build for later checking.
  9. Coordinator: I'll keep the resolved category for issues whose stated resolution has supporting evidence.
  10. Tester: Then readers can distinguish an open issue deliberately postponed from a defect actually removed.

Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Original fictional case: An export defect omits the final row in one monthly workflow. A heading typo appears on every account page. Tomorrow's customer demonstration uses the account page, with no export planned. Tester Lina and product lead Marcus discuss triage. The export has a data-completeness impact; the typo has broad visibility and immediate demonstration relevance. No severity scale, final repair order, or approval to release either issue is supplied.
Language workshop: The missing export row and tomorrow's visible typo
Communication goal: Compare functional impact, reach, and timing without equating demonstration urgency with defect severity or release approval.
Language guidance: Monthly describes when the export workflow occurs; it does not make a missing row trivial. Every account page describes the typo's reach; it does not make its functional impact identical to missing export data.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The export omits its final row. / The observed issue affects the monthly workflow. / The heading typo appears on every account page. / The demonstration uses the account page. / No export is planned for the demonstration. / Impact and scheduling urgency are different questions. / Monthly use does not make missing data harmless. / Broad visibility does not automatically mean greater functional harm. / The demonstration makes the typo immediately relevant. / Which severity definitions apply here? / We have not agreed the repair order. / Triage discussion is not release approval. / Please record the disposition for each issue. / We have not established an alternative export method. / Keep data completeness and presentation visibility in the comparison. / Discuss impact, timing, and authorization separately.
Vocabulary: triage: Structured review to classify issues and decide next handling. / severity: Degree of impact caused by a defect under the relevant scale. / priority: Relative urgency or order assigned to work. / functional impact: Effect on the ability to perform the intended task. / visibility: Extent to which users encounter or notice an issue. / reach: Range of users or surfaces affected. / frequency: How often the relevant event or workflow occurs. / data completeness: Presence of all required data items. / cosmetic defect: Presentation issue without an established functional failure here. / customer demonstration: Planned presentation of product behavior to a customer. / business timing: Importance arising from an event or deadline. / repair order: Sequence in which issues are scheduled for correction. / release blocker: Issue preventing release under the applicable criteria. / waiver: Explicit authorized acceptance of an exception. / risk acceptance: Authorized decision to proceed with identified exposure. / workaround: Alternative way to complete a task despite a defect. / affected workflow: Process in which the reported issue occurs. / stakeholder impact: Consequence for people who rely on the product. / defect classification: Assignment of an issue to a defined category. / scheduling constraint: Limit affecting when work can be performed. / decision owner: Person responsible for a specified decision. / evidence-based comparison: Comparison grounded in observed effects and stated context. / defer: Postpone work without claiming the issue is resolved. / disposition: Recorded decision about how an issue will be handled.
Speaking task: Read the triage comparison once for functional impact, then again for demonstration timing. Swap roles for turns 9-16. Keep classification, work order, and permission to release separate. Repeat the closing status without adding an invented workaround, severity scale, or approved repair order.
Partner: Lina: Tester / Marcus: Product lead
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Complete the triage summary without assigning an unsupported rating or release decision.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Lina: My comparison will show the missing row, the typo's reach, and the demonstration's actual scope. It won't invent consequences we haven't observed.
Marcus: That is an evidence-based comparison. It gives the team a clear basis for scheduling discussion without pretending a decision has already been reached.
Lina: I'll leave the repair order and release disposition pending. Strongly arguing for urgency doesn't mean I can announce an authorization.
Marcus: Good. Keep any risk acceptance separate and explicit. We can discuss urgency strongly without silently approving either defect for release.
END REFERENCE
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

07Reporting exploratory findingsSaved edits persist; unsaved edits disappear silently+

Your goal

Report a timeboxed exploratory observation while separating behavior, an unresolved warning requirement, and untested mobile scope.

This lesson in the book, page 58 ↗

A Read the situation

Saved edits persist; unsaved edits disappear silently

Tester Isha completes a twenty-minute exploratory session with the charter Investigate interrupted profile edits. Navigating away after a saved edit preserves the value. Navigating away during an unsaved edit loses it without warning. Whether a warning is required is unspecified. Mobile behavior was not explored. Isha debriefs with product analyst Owen, who needs a factual record and a clarification request rather than a claim that every profile edit is broken.

Isha: Exploratory tester / Owen: Product analyst

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What was the session's charter?

Answer and explanation

A. Investigate interrupted profile edits

The stated exploratory goal concerns interruption during profile editing.

2. What happens after a saved edit?

Answer and explanation

B. Navigating away preserves the value

The saved-edit observation shows persistence after navigating away.

3. What is unresolved?

Answer and explanation

D. Whether a warning is required for unsaved edits

Warning requirements are unspecified even though the no-warning behavior was observed.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

The saved and unsaved paths have different observations. Saved edits persist after navigation; unsaved edits are lost without warning. Do not merge them into all edits are lost or assume that loss of an unsaved change violates an unstated rule.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
exploratory testing
Investigation that adapts checks as the tester learns.conduct exploratory testing
test charter
Statement guiding the purpose of an exploratory session.define the test charter
focus containment
Keeping keyboard tab navigation within an open modal dialog.verify focus containment
timebox
Fixed time allocated to an investigation.respect the timebox
debrief
Discussion reviewing observations, limits, and follow-up.hold a debrief
session notes
Record of actions, observations, and questions from testing.preserve session notes
observation
Behavior directly seen during the investigation.separate observation from interpretation
finding
Recorded result or question arising from investigation.report a finding
saved state
Data retained after a completed save under the observed behavior.verify saved state
unsaved change
Edit not yet committed by the relevant save action.identify an unsaved change
navigation away
Leaving the current view or page.describe navigation away
persistence
Continued retention of data across the relevant transition.check persistence
warning prompt
Message alerting a user before a consequential action.clarify the warning prompt
state transition
Change from one defined condition to another.record the state transition
user journey
Sequence of actions a user takes toward an objective.describe the user journey
focus restoration
Returning keyboard focus to the required target after an interface closes.check focus restoration
requirement gap
Missing specification needed to judge intended behavior.raise the requirement gap
usability concern
Potential difficulty in understanding or using the product.record a usability concern
confirmed defect
Established departure from an applicable expectation.distinguish a confirmed defect
follow-up question
Specific unresolved point for further investigation or clarification.record a follow-up question
scope exclusion
Area explicitly not covered by the investigation.list a scope exclusion
mobile coverage
Evidence from checking behavior on mobile devices or conditions.avoid assuming mobile coverage
evidence trail
Records linking a finding to actions and observations.retain the evidence trail
session outcome
Bounded summary of findings and remaining questions.summarize the session outcome

Record the no-warning behavior and request a decision on whether a warning is required. Mobile was not explored, and twenty minutes is the session duration, not evidence of complete profile-edit coverage.

C Notice the language

An observation can be important before classification

Describe the exact sequence and what happened, then ask whether the intended behavior requires a warning. This preserves a potentially important user experience without inventing a requirement. Naming untested scope helps colleagues use the finding appropriately.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Name the charter
The session investigated interrupted profile edits.
State the timebox
I explored for twenty minutes.
Separate the saved path
After saving, navigation preserved the value.
Separate the unsaved path
Navigating away during an unsaved edit lost the change.
Describe the interface
No warning appeared in that sequence.
Avoid a broad defect claim
I did not observe all profile edits failing.
Expose the requirement gap
The warning requirement is unspecified.
Request the intended behavior
Should leaving an unsaved edit trigger a warning?
Keep the observation
The no-warning behavior remains a useful finding.
Avoid inventing policy
I have not assumed that every unsaved edit must persist.
State the excluded scope
I did not explore mobile behavior.
Request traceable follow-up
Link the warning decision to the session notes.
Separate classification
The observation is not yet a confirmed requirement violation.
Preserve the sequence
Record whether save occurred before navigation.
Limit the conclusion
These findings apply to the paths explored.
Close the debrief
Saved value persists; unsaved change is lost without warning; requirement and mobile questions remain.
6 language and precision notes
After versus during
Distinguishes navigation following save from navigation during an unsaved edit.
No warning appeared
Reports an observation without asserting an unstated requirement.
Should ... trigger
Requests the intended behavior as an explicit decision.
Not yet confirmed
Preserves a finding while qualifying its classification.
Did not explore
Names an evidence gap without predicting the result.
Whether save occurred
Identifies the state distinction needed to reproduce the observation.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which report matches the observations?

Answer and explanation

D. Saved edits persist; unsaved edits are lost without warning

The two stated navigation sequences have different outcomes that must remain separate.

2. What should be requested?

Answer and explanation

C. Clarification of whether an unsaved-edit warning is required

The missing rule concerns the intended warning behavior for unsaved navigation.

3. Which claim exceeds the evidence?

Answer and explanation

D. Mobile shows the same result

Mobile was explicitly not explored, so its behavior is unknown.

4. Why avoid calling this a confirmed warning defect now?

Answer and explanation

C. The applicable warning requirement is unspecified

A confirmed requirement violation needs an applicable expectation, which is missing for the warning.

Answers and explanations
  1. test charter Turn 2. Test charter names the session's purpose, investigating interrupted profile edits, rather than a claim of complete feature coverage.
  2. saved state Turn 4. Saved state refers to the value retained after the completed save, which persisted through the observed navigation.
  3. observation Turn 6. Observation reports the actual loss of the unsaved change without warning, without inventing an intended-behavior rule.
  4. requirement gap Turn 8. Requirement gap identifies the missing warning rule needed to classify the observed behavior against intended expectations.
  5. evidence trail Turn 10. Evidence trail connects the question to the recorded action sequence and outcome so another person can investigate it.
  6. usability concern Turn 12. Usability concern can flag a potentially confusing user experience before an unspecified warning policy is resolved.
  7. scope exclusion Turn 14. Scope exclusion records that mobile behavior was not explored and prevents the observed result being generalized to it.
  8. timebox Turn 16. Timebox is the twenty-minute duration of the investigation, not a measure proving complete coverage of interruptions.
  9. confirmed defect Turn 18. Confirmed defect requires evidence of a departure from an applicable expectation, which the missing warning rule does not yet provide.
  10. session outcome Turn 20. Session outcome summarizes the explored behaviors and remaining questions without claiming all profile edits or mobile behavior were tested.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Unsaved edits disappear without a warning

A fictional tester debriefs the twenty-minute interrupted-edit session.

  1. Tester: The charter focused on interrupted profile edits for twenty minutes, using the desktop view.
  2. Designer: What did you learn about saved values compared with edits still in progress?
  3. Tester: Saved values survived navigation. Unsaved edits disappeared without a warning in the path I explored.
  4. Designer: Do we have an acceptance criterion requiring a warning before that navigation?
  5. Tester: Not yet. I'm reporting a usability concern for clarification, not a confirmed requirement mismatch.
  6. Designer: That distinction is useful. We need to agree what users should expect before labeling the verdict.
  7. Tester: Mobile behavior remains unexplored, so these observations should not be presented as cross-device findings.
  8. Designer: Did you follow a particular clue that changed the direction of the session?
  9. Tester: Yes. The contrast between saved and unsaved state led me to compare both navigation paths.
  10. Designer: Then the debrief identifies what you learned, why you investigated it, and which expectation remains open.
A charter narrows exploration without scripting every step

Fictional testers agree a bounded session on interrupted searches.

  1. Test lead: Your test charter says explore search. That's broad for fifteen minutes. What question matters most?
  2. Tester: I want to investigate whether changing filters during a pending search produces understandable results.
  3. Test lead: That gives the session a focus without prescribing every action before you see what happens.
  4. Tester: I'll use interruption and repeated actions as heuristics to suggest relevant variations during exploration.
  5. Test lead: Please treat those as thinking aids, not guarantees that any unexpected result is a defect.
  6. Tester: Agreed. I'll compare observations with the stated filter behavior and flag unclear expectations separately.
  7. Test lead: What will your debrief distinguish if the timebox ends while an interesting clue is still open?
  8. Tester: The paths actually tried, the observed concern, and the next question that wasn't investigated.
  9. Test lead: Good. Fifteen minutes bounds effort; it doesn't mean the search feature becomes fully covered.
  10. Tester: I'll use the narrower charter and keep the unexamined paths visible when reporting the session.
An unexpected label becomes a focused inquiry

A fictional exploratory tester discusses a clue rather than merely continuing random actions.

  1. Tester: During exploration, the confirmation label named the previous profile after switching accounts.
  2. Developer: Was the saved data wrong too, or have you only observed the label so far?
  3. Tester: Only the label. I paused the broader charter to compare the current profile and the displayed name.
  4. Developer: What expectation supports treating that label as a defect rather than simply unfamiliar wording?
  5. Tester: The current criterion says the confirmation names the profile just saved, which differs from this observation.
  6. Developer: Then you have a specific mismatch. Please preserve the account-switch sequence and both profile references.
  7. Tester: My invented profiles Blue and Green let us reproduce this without real account information.
  8. Developer: Does the evidence establish whether storage also used the wrong profile, or is that still untested?
  9. Tester: Still untested. The label finding should not become a claim about a data update I didn't inspect.
  10. Developer: That leaves a reproducible presentation defect and a separate stored-state question.

Actual requirements, test policies, evidence standards, privacy safeguards, and release authority take precedence. The exercises authorize no live-system changes. Lesson 07: Reporting exploratory findings
Original fictional case: Tester Isha completes a twenty-minute exploratory session with the charter Investigate interrupted profile edits. Navigating away after a saved edit preserves the value. Navigating away during an unsaved edit loses it without warning. Whether a warning is required is unspecified. Mobile behavior was not explored. Isha debriefs with product analyst Owen, who needs a factual record and a clarification request rather than a claim that every profile edit is broken.
Language workshop: Saved edits persist; unsaved edits disappear silently
Communication goal: Report a timeboxed exploratory observation while separating behavior, an unresolved warning requirement, and untested mobile scope.
Language guidance: The saved and unsaved paths have different observations. Saved edits persist after navigation; unsaved edits are lost without warning. Do not merge them into all edits are lost or assume that loss of an unsaved change violates an unstated rule.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The session investigated interrupted profile edits. / I explored for twenty minutes. / After saving, navigation preserved the value. / Navigating away during an unsaved edit lost the change. / No warning appeared in that sequence. / I did not observe all profile edits failing. / The warning requirement is unspecified. / Should leaving an unsaved edit trigger a warning? / The no-warning behavior remains a useful finding. / I have not assumed that every unsaved edit must persist. / I did not explore mobile behavior. / Link the warning decision to the session notes. / The observation is not yet a confirmed requirement violation. / Record whether save occurred before navigation. / These findings apply to the paths explored. / Saved value persists; unsaved change is lost without warning; requirement and mobile questions remain.
Vocabulary: exploratory testing: Investigation that adapts checks as the tester learns. / test charter: Statement guiding the purpose of an exploratory session. / focus containment: Keeping keyboard tab navigation within an open modal dialog. / timebox: Fixed time allocated to an investigation. / debrief: Discussion reviewing observations, limits, and follow-up. / session notes: Record of actions, observations, and questions from testing. / observation: Behavior directly seen during the investigation. / finding: Recorded result or question arising from investigation. / saved state: Data retained after a completed save under the observed behavior. / unsaved change: Edit not yet committed by the relevant save action. / navigation away: Leaving the current view or page. / persistence: Continued retention of data across the relevant transition. / warning prompt: Message alerting a user before a consequential action. / state transition: Change from one defined condition to another. / user journey: Sequence of actions a user takes toward an objective. / focus restoration: Returning keyboard focus to the required target after an interface closes. / requirement gap: Missing specification needed to judge intended behavior. / usability concern: Potential difficulty in understanding or using the product. / confirmed defect: Established departure from an applicable expectation. / follow-up question: Specific unresolved point for further investigation or clarification. / scope exclusion: Area explicitly not covered by the investigation. / mobile coverage: Evidence from checking behavior on mobile devices or conditions. / evidence trail: Records linking a finding to actions and observations. / session outcome: Bounded summary of findings and remaining questions.
Speaking task: Read the saved and unsaved navigation sequences separately, with the observed result after each. Swap roles for turns 7-14. Keep the warning requirement unresolved and mobile behavior untested. Check the final debrief against the supplied observations, not a broad claim that all profile edits fail.
Partner: Isha: Exploratory tester / Owen: Product analyst
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Complete the exploratory report without converting an observation into an unsupported requirement judgment.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Isha: After the owner decides the warning rule, we can test against it. Until then the original observation stays attached to the clarification request.
Owen: That keeps confirmed defect separate from an unresolved finding. The eventual decision may change the classification without changing what you actually observed.
Isha: My summary will retain saved-value persistence, unsaved loss without warning, the missing warning rule, and no mobile coverage.
Owen: Good. That is a bounded session outcome. It gives the next reviewer concrete evidence and one answerable follow-up question, rather than a broad label they have to reconstruct.
END REFERENCE
Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

08Giving a release-evidence handoverSeven passed is not ten completed+

Your goal

Report pass, failure, and blocked counts while identifying the unmet critical-path criterion and preserving release ownership.

This lesson in the book, page 67 ↗

A Read the situation

Seven passed is not ten completed

Fictional candidate Q20 has ten planned checks. Seven passed, one failed, and two are blocked by unavailable sample data. One blocked check is critical. The agreed exit criterion requires every critical-path check to be executed and reviewed. Tester Ben hands the evidence to release owner Mara. The critical blocked check means this criterion is not met. Mara has not made the release decision; the test summary is not approval.

Ben: Tester preparing the handover / Mara: Release decision owner

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. How are the ten checks divided?

Answer and explanation

D. Seven passed, one failed, two blocked

The stated totals are seven passes, one failure, and two blocked checks.

2. Why is the critical-path criterion unmet?

Answer and explanation

D. One critical check is blocked and unexecuted

The criterion requires execution and review of every critical-path check, including the blocked one.

3. Who owns the release decision?

Answer and explanation

D. Mara

Mara is explicitly named as the owner of the unresolved release decision.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Seven plus one plus two accounts for all ten planned checks. Eight have executed; two have not. Seven out of ten planned is 70 percent, while seven out of eight executed is 87.5 percent. Neither percentage makes the critical blocked check disappear.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
release candidate
Version proposed for release pending the relevant decision.identify the release candidate
planned check
Test included in the intended checking scope.count planned checks
passed check
Executed check meeting its expected result.report passed checks
failed check
Executed check not meeting its expected result.report the failed check
blocked check
Check unable to proceed because a prerequisite is unavailable.identify blocked checks
sample data
Prepared input needed to execute a test.obtain sample data
critical path
Essential workflow or route under the agreed release criteria here.identify the critical path
exit criterion
Condition required before leaving a testing stage.assess the exit criterion
execution status
Whether and how a test has been run.distinguish execution status
review status
Whether relevant evidence has received the required review.report review status
test completion
End-of-stage accounting of performed work and unresolved items.assess test completion
pass rate
Proportion of passes under a stated denominator.qualify the pass rate
denominator
Total used to calculate a fraction or percentage.specify the denominator
blocked dependency
Missing prerequisite preventing a check from proceeding.resolve the blocked dependency
open defect
Unresolved product issue recorded for follow-up.retain the open defect
readiness evidence
Results and records relevant to whether criteria are satisfied.present readiness evidence
release authority
Permission or role empowered to decide release.respect release authority
decision pending
Status showing that a decision has not been made.keep decision pending
status breakdown
Separate counts or categories explaining the overall position.give the status breakdown
evidence handover
Transfer of results, limits, and unresolved dependencies.prepare the evidence handover
criterion exception
Explicit authorized departure from an agreed criterion.document a criterion exception
risk register
Record of identified risks and their handling.update the risk register
unexecuted check
Planned check that has not actually run.disclose the unexecuted check
release disposition
Recorded decision on whether and how to proceed.record the release disposition

The agreed criterion is unmet because a critical check has not executed and therefore cannot have its result reviewed. A summary must say this directly. No exception or release approval has been granted, and Mara retains the pending decision.

C Notice the language

Do not let a percentage hide a blocker

A headline pass count can sound reassuring while omitting an unexecuted critical path. Give the complete breakdown, explain the relevant criterion, and name the missing evidence. Keep your report of readiness conditions separate from the authorized release decision.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Identify the candidate
This handover concerns Q20.
State the total
Ten checks were planned.
Give passes
Seven checks passed.
Give failures
One check failed.
Give blockers
Two checks are blocked by unavailable sample data.
Identify the critical gap
One blocked check is critical.
State the criterion
All critical-path checks must be executed and reviewed.
State the consequence
That criterion is not currently met.
Separate blocked and failed
Blocked does not mean executed and failed.
Separate blocked and passed
Missing data cannot count as a pass.
Qualify the percentage
Name whether the denominator is planned or executed checks.
Preserve the failure
Keep the failed check visible alongside the blockers.
Request the dependency
The blocked checks need their sample data.
Name authority
Mara owns the release decision.
Avoid implying approval
The decision remains pending.
Close the handover
Report the full breakdown, unmet criterion, and next evidence needed.
6 language and precision notes
Planned versus executed
Distinguishes ten intended checks from the eight that actually ran.
Currently
States the present criterion status without predicting the final decision.
By unavailable data
Names the dependency causing the blocked status.
Must be ... and
Preserves both execution and review requirements.
Alongside
Keeps failures visible rather than reporting only blockers.
Remains pending
Makes clear that a report is not an authorization.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. How many checks have executed?

Answer and explanation

C. Eight

Seven passed checks plus one failed check equals eight executed checks.

2. Which pair states pass proportions with matching denominators?

Answer and explanation

A. 70% of planned; 87.5% of executed

Seven divided by ten planned is 70%; seven divided by eight executed is 87.5%. Neither rate satisfies the missing critical-path criterion.

3. Which criterion statement is justified?

Answer and explanation

C. It is unmet because a critical check remains unexecuted

Every critical-path check must execute and be reviewed, and one remains blocked.

4. Which handover is complete?

Answer and explanation

A. Q20: seven passed, one failed, two blocked including one critical; criterion unmet; Mara's decision pending

The complete handover includes all statuses, the critical gap, criterion consequence, and decision ownership.

Check explanations in the book, page 99 ↗

E Conversations

Seven passed is not ten completed

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Ben: Tester preparing the handover / Mara: Release decision owner

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • denominator
  • failed check
  • blocked dependency
  • blocked check
  • status breakdown
  • Review status
  • execution status
  • release authority
  • readiness evidence
  • exit criterion
  1. Ben
    Q20 has ten planned checks: seven passed, one failed, two blocked by unavailable sample data. One of the blocked checks is critical.
  2. Mara
    Keep that 1 in the headline. A pass percentage alone won't tell me whether our critical-path condition has been met.
  3. Ben
    It hasn't. Every critical-path check must execute and have its evidence reviewed, and the blocked one has no result to review.
  4. Mara
    Then state the 2 is unmet. A majority of passes doesn't substitute for that missing critical evidence.
  5. Ben
    I'll keep blocked separate from failed. Those two never executed, so there's no result showing either success or an expectation mismatch.
  6. Mara
    Correct. A 3 isn't a pass by default, and it isn't an executed failure. Name why it couldn't proceed.
  7. Ben
    Eight ran: seven passes plus one failure. Ten is the planned total, not the number completed.
  8. Mara
    That makes 4 unambiguous. The remaining two still need their prerequisites before they can run.
  9. Ben
    Seven over ten planned is seventy percent. Seven over eight executed is eighty-seven-point-five. Both leave the critical blocker unresolved.
  10. Mara
    Exactly. Every rate needs its 5. A higher-looking percentage doesn't change what actually happened.
  11. Ben
    I'll retain the failed result alongside the blockers. Otherwise the missing-data discussion could overshadow a defect we actually observed.
  12. Mara
    Yes. The 6 needs its own follow-up. It shouldn't vanish when the report focuses on enabling the unexecuted checks.
  13. Ben
    The data request is still pending. I won't write data received or assume the tests will pass once somebody supplies it.
  14. Mara
    Keep that 7 explicit. Requesting a prerequisite, receiving it, and executing a check are separate stages.
  15. Ben
    And even after execution, the criterion still requires review. I'll show that separately instead of copying completed into every column.
  16. Mara
    Good. 8 can't be inferred from a test run. Account for both parts of the requirement.
  17. Ben
    The report will say criterion unmet, no granted exception, and your production decision pending. This handoff isn't a sign-off.
  18. Mara
    That respects 9. I own the decision, but I haven't made it or authorized an exception.
  19. Ben
    Final status: Q20, seven passed, one failed, two blocked including one critical, missing data, required review, and decision pending.
  20. Mara
    That's usable 10. It supports an explicit decision without concealing the failed check or implying the blocked critical path has passed.

Answers and explanations
  1. status breakdown Turn 2. Status breakdown accounts separately for all ten planned checks instead of hiding failure and blockers behind the pass count.
  2. exit criterion Turn 4. Exit criterion requires execution and review of every critical-path check, which the blocked critical check prevents us from satisfying.
  3. blocked check Turn 6. Blocked check means execution is prevented by a missing prerequisite, not that the expected result was met or violated.
  4. execution status Turn 8. Execution status separates the eight checks that ran from the two that could not run due to missing data.
  5. denominator Turn 10. Denominator determines whether the reported pass proportion uses ten planned checks or eight executed checks.
  6. failed check Turn 12. Failed check records an executed expectation mismatch and must remain visible separately from the unavailable-data blockers.
  7. blocked dependency Turn 14. Blocked dependency is the unavailable sample data that must be resolved before the affected checks can execute.
  8. Review status Turn 16. Review status is a separate requirement from execution under the stated criterion, so running a check alone is not sufficient.
  9. release authority Turn 18. Release authority remains with Mara, and the evidence report does not itself grant approval or an exception.
  10. readiness evidence Turn 20. Readiness evidence presents actual results and unmet conditions for the decision maker without claiming release approval.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Ben: Q20 has ten planned checks: seven passed, one failed, two blocked by unavailable sample data. One of the blocked checks is critical.
  2. Mara: Keep that status breakdown in the headline. A pass percentage alone won't tell me whether our critical-path condition has been met.
  3. Ben: It hasn't. Every critical-path check must execute and have its evidence reviewed, and the blocked one has no result to review.
  4. Mara: Then state the exit criterion is unmet. A majority of passes doesn't substitute for that missing critical evidence.
  5. Ben: I'll keep blocked separate from failed. Those two never executed, so there's no result showing either success or an expectation mismatch.
  6. Mara: Correct. A blocked check isn't a pass by default, and it isn't an executed failure. Name why it couldn't proceed.
  7. Ben: Eight ran: seven passes plus one failure. Ten is the planned total, not the number completed.
  8. Mara: That makes execution status unambiguous. The remaining two still need their prerequisites before they can run.
  9. Ben: Seven over ten planned is seventy percent. Seven over eight executed is eighty-seven-point-five. Both leave the critical blocker unresolved.
  10. Mara: Exactly. Every rate needs its denominator. A higher-looking percentage doesn't change what actually happened.
  11. Ben: I'll retain the failed result alongside the blockers. Otherwise the missing-data discussion could overshadow a defect we actually observed.
  12. Mara: Yes. The failed check needs its own follow-up. It shouldn't vanish when the report focuses on enabling the unexecuted checks.
  13. Ben: The data request is still pending. I won't write data received or assume the tests will pass once somebody supplies it.
  14. Mara: Keep that blocked dependency explicit. Requesting a prerequisite, receiving it, and executing a check are separate stages.
  15. Ben: And even after execution, the criterion still requires review. I'll show that separately instead of copying completed into every column.
  16. Mara: Good. Review status can't be inferred from a test run. Account for both parts of the requirement.
  17. Ben: The report will say criterion unmet, no granted exception, and your production decision pending. This handoff isn't a sign-off.
  18. Mara: That respects release authority. I own the decision, but I haven't made it or authorized an exception.
  19. Ben: Final status: Q20, seven passed, one failed, two blocked including one critical, missing data, required review, and decision pending.
  20. Mara: That's usable readiness evidence. It supports an explicit decision without concealing the failed check or implying the blocked critical path has passed.
Dialogue in the book, page 73 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Seven passed, one failed, two blocked

A fictional tester hands Q20 evidence to release owner Mara.

  1. Tester: Q20 has ten planned checks: seven passed, one failed, and two were blocked by unavailable sample data.
  2. Mara: Does either blocked test sit on the critical path named in our exit criterion?
  3. Tester: Yes, one does. The required execution and review of all critical checks is therefore incomplete.
  4. Mara: Then the passing majority doesn't mean that criterion has been met for the candidate.
  5. Tester: Correct. Please take the failed result and blocked critical check into your release decision.
  6. Mara: I accept the evidence handover. Has anyone already described this summary as release approval?
  7. Tester: Not in the record. I will keep the testing status distinct from your pending decision.
  8. Mara: Who owns obtaining the sample data so we can address the blocked check?
  9. Tester: The test-data coordinator has accepted that request; availability is not yet confirmed.
  10. Mara: Then I'll review the current residual risk without treating unavailable evidence as a passing result.
Seven out of eight hides the planned scope

A fictional test lead clarifies a pass-rate statement before a release meeting.

  1. Coordinator: I plan to report seven passes out of eight executed checks. Is that accurate?
  2. Test lead: The executed denominator is accurate, but the plan has ten checks, including two blocked ones.
  3. Coordinator: So I should present both figures rather than let listeners assume eight was the full scope.
  4. Test lead: Yes. One blocked check is critical, which matters more than the attractive pass percentage alone.
  5. Coordinator: Would calling blocked tests failed be a conservative way to keep that risk visible?
  6. Test lead: No. It would misstate the evidence. Blocked means the intended behavior couldn't be evaluated.
  7. Coordinator: Then the summary should state seven passed, one failed, two blocked, and the unmet critical criterion.
  8. Test lead: Exactly. Clear categories preserve both the observed failure and the uncertainty caused by missing execution.
  9. Coordinator: I'll use that distribution and leave the release decision with the named owner.
  10. Test lead: That gives the meeting a faithful test summary, without overstating what a percentage means.
Yesterday's evidence belongs to yesterday's candidate

A fictional tester qualifies a test summary after a new build arrives.

  1. Release owner: The report is green, but a new candidate arrived this morning. Which build does the summary cover?
  2. Tester: It covers P7 through yesterday afternoon. Today's P8 isn't included in that evidence cutoff.
  3. Release owner: Can we simply change the heading if the release note describes only a minor adjustment?
  4. Tester: No. The old results remain P7 evidence until P8's differences and required checks are reviewed.
  5. Release owner: Then the summary shouldn't imply P8 passed simply because its name is similar.
  6. Tester: Correct. I'll retain P7's results and identify P8's current testing status separately as not yet evaluated.
  7. Release owner: Who will review the change impact and decide which prior evidence remains relevant?
  8. Tester: The test lead will assess that with the developer, using the actual difference between the builds.
  9. Release owner: Bring that bounded assessment to release review rather than discard or relabel old evidence.
  10. Tester: I'll do that. Useful historical evidence can remain useful without becoming a result for an untested candidate.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Seven passed is not ten completed
  1. Read the Q20 status counts aloud and verify that seven plus one plus two accounts for ten planned checks.
  2. Swap roles for turns 7-16. Say the denominator with each percentage and keep execution separate from review.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Account for every planned check

Complete the Q20 handover. The release decision has not been made.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Tester: "___ checks passed."

Answer and explanation

C. Seven

The scenario establishes seven passed checks out of the ten planned.

2. Owner: "___ check failed."

Answer and explanation

B. One

One executed check failed and must remain distinct from blocked checks.

3. Tester: "___ checks are blocked by missing sample data."

Answer and explanation

A. Two

Two checks could not execute because their required sample data was unavailable.

4. Owner: "The release decision remains ___."

Answer and explanation

B. pending

Mara has not made the release decision, and the report is not approval.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

REFERENCE
Course: Software Quality Assurance and Testing English
Professional audience: Software quality assurance analysts, manual testers, test engineers, automation engineers, and test leads
Scope: All products, builds, requirements, observations, and decisions are fictional. This book teaches workplace English, not certification content or a complete testing method. Actual requirements, test policies, evidence standards, privacy safeguards, and release authority take precedence. The exercises authorize no live-system changes. A proposed check has no result until executed, and an observed result applies only to its stated conditions.
Lesson 08: Giving a release-evidence handover
Original fictional case: Fictional candidate Q20 has ten planned checks. Seven passed, one failed, and two are blocked by unavailable sample data. One blocked check is critical. The agreed exit criterion requires every critical-path check to be executed and reviewed. Tester Ben hands the evidence to release owner Mara. The critical blocked check means this criterion is not met. Mara has not made the release decision; the test summary is not approval.
Language workshop: Seven passed is not ten completed
Communication goal: Report pass, failure, and blocked counts while identifying the unmet critical-path criterion and preserving release ownership.
Language guidance: Seven plus one plus two accounts for all ten planned checks. Eight have executed; two have not. Seven out of ten planned is 70 percent, while seven out of eight executed is 87.5 percent. Neither percentage makes the critical blocked check disappear.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): This handover concerns Q20. / Ten checks were planned. / Seven checks passed. / One check failed. / Two checks are blocked by unavailable sample data. / One blocked check is critical. / All critical-path checks must be executed and reviewed. / That criterion is not currently met. / Blocked does not mean executed and failed. / Missing data cannot count as a pass. / Name whether the denominator is planned or executed checks. / Keep the failed check visible alongside the blockers. / The blocked checks need their sample data. / Mara owns the release decision. / The decision remains pending. / Report the full breakdown, unmet criterion, and next evidence needed.
Vocabulary: release candidate: Version proposed for release pending the relevant decision. / planned check: Test included in the intended checking scope. / passed check: Executed check meeting its expected result. / failed check: Executed check not meeting its expected result. / blocked check: Check unable to proceed because a prerequisite is unavailable. / sample data: Prepared input needed to execute a test. / critical path: Essential workflow or route under the agreed release criteria here. / exit criterion: Condition required before leaving a testing stage. / execution status: Whether and how a test has been run. / review status: Whether relevant evidence has received the required review. / test completion: End-of-stage accounting of performed work and unresolved items. / pass rate: Proportion of passes under a stated denominator. / denominator: Total used to calculate a fraction or percentage. / blocked dependency: Missing prerequisite preventing a check from proceeding. / open defect: Unresolved product issue recorded for follow-up. / readiness evidence: Results and records relevant to whether criteria are satisfied. / release authority: Permission or role empowered to decide release. / decision pending: Status showing that a decision has not been made. / status breakdown: Separate counts or categories explaining the overall position. / evidence handover: Transfer of results, limits, and unresolved dependencies. / criterion exception: Explicit authorized departure from an agreed criterion. / risk register: Record of identified risks and their handling. / unexecuted check: Planned check that has not actually run. / release disposition: Recorded decision on whether and how to proceed.
Speaking task: Read the Q20 status counts aloud and verify that seven plus one plus two accounts for ten planned checks. Swap roles for turns 7-16. Say the denominator with each percentage and keep execution separate from review. Repeat the closing handoff. Retain the unmet critical-path criterion and Mara's pending decision.
Partner: Ben: Tester preparing the handover / Mara: Release decision owner
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Complete the Q20 handover. The release decision has not been made.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Ben: The report will say criterion unmet, no granted exception, and your production decision pending. This handoff isn't a sign-off.
Mara: That respects release authority. I own the decision, but I haven't made it or authorized an exception.
Ben: Final status: Q20, seven passed, one failed, two blocked including one critical, missing data, required review, and decision pending.
Mara: That's usable readiness evidence. It supports an explicit decision without concealing the failed check or implying the blocked critical path has passed.
END REFERENCE
Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.
